Bordeaux, CNRS, INRIA, Bordeaux INP, IMB, UMR 5251, F-33400 Talence, France 22institutetext: ENS de Lyon, CNRS, UMPA, UMR 5669, Lyon, France The supersingular Endomorphism Ring and One Endomorphism problems are equivalent Aurel Page 11 Benjamin Wesolowski 22 Abstract The supersingular Endomorphism Ring problem is the following: given a supersingular elliptic curve, compute all of its endomorphisms. The presumed hardness of this problem is foundational for isogeny-based cryptography. The One Endomorphism problem only asks to find a single non-scalar endomorphism. We prove that these two problems are equivalent, under probabilistic polynomial time reductions. We prove a number of consequences. First, assuming the hardness of the endomorphism ring problem, the Charles–Goren–Lauter hash function is collision resistant, and the SQIsign identification protocol is sound. Second, the endomorphism ring problem is equivalent to the problem of computing arbitrary isogenies between supersingular elliptic curves, a result previously known only for isogenies of smooth degree. Third, there exists an unconditional probabilistic algorithm to solve the endomorphism ring problem in time O~⁢(p1/2)~𝑂superscript𝑝12\tilde{O}(p^{1/2})over~ start_ARG italic_O end_ARG ( italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 1 / 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ), a result that previously required to assume the generalized Riemann hypothesis. To prove our main result, we introduce a flexible framework for the study of isogeny graphs with additional information. We prove a general and easy-to-use rapid mixing theorem. 1 Introduction The endomorphism ring problem lies at the foundation of isogeny-based cryptography. On one hand, its presumed hardness is necessary for the security of all cryptosystems of this family (see for instance the reductions in [Wes22a]). On the other hand, many cryptosystems of this family can be proven secure if this problem (or some variant) is hard (the earliest example being [CLG09]). Isogeny-based cryptography takes its name from the isogeny problem. An isogeny is a certain kind of map between two elliptic curves, and the isogeny problem consists in finding such a map, given the two curves. Formalising the meaning of “finding an isogeny” can lead to several versions of the isogeny problem, the most prominent being the ℓnormal-ℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny path problem. In isogeny-based cryptography, one typically restricts to supersingular elliptic curves, for which this problem is believed to be hard. Fix a supersingular elliptic curve E𝐸Eitalic_E. An endomorphism of E𝐸Eitalic_E is an isogeny from E𝐸Eitalic_E to itself (or the zero morphism). The collection of all endomorphisms of E𝐸Eitalic_E forms the endomorphism ring End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ). The supersingular endomorphism ring problem, or EndRing, consists in computing End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ), when given E𝐸Eitalic_E. Assuming the generalised Riemann hypothesis, this problem is equivalent to the ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny path problem (see [Wes22b], and the earlier heuristic equivalence [EHL+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT18]), cementing its importance in the field. The endomorphism ring contains scalars 𝐙⊆End⁡(E)𝐙End𝐸\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}\subseteq\operatorname{End}(E)bold_Z ⊆ roman_End ( italic_E ), simple elements which are always easy to compute. While EndRing asks to find all endomorphisms, it has long been believed that finding even a single non-scalar endomorphism is hard. We call this the one endomorphism problem, or OneEnd. Unfortunately, former heuristic arguments suggesting that OneEnd should be as hard as EndRing do not withstand close scrutiny, and actually fail in simple cases. Yet, the connection between these two problems bears important consequences on the hardness of EndRing, on its connection with variants of the isogeny problem, and on the security of cryptosystems such as the CGL hash function [CLG09] or the SQIsign digital signature scheme [DKL+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT20]. 1.1 Contributions In this article, we prove the following theorem. Theorem 1.1 The EndRing and OneEnd problems are equivalent, under probabilistic polynomial time reductions. Formal definitions are provided in Section 2, and the proof is the object of Section 7. The reduction from OneEnd to EndRing is obvious, and the other direction is stated more precisely in Theorem 7.1. As a consequence of the main theorem, we prove the following: • If EndRing is hard, then the CGL hash function is collision resistant (Theorem 8.1), and the SQIsign identification scheme is sound (Theorem 8.2). Previous security proofs relied on the hardness of OneEnd (see [DKL+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT20, Theorem 1]), or on flawed heuristic reductions (see [EHL+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT18, Algorithm 8], and the flaws discussed Section 1.2). This is the object of Section 8.1 and Section 8.2. • EndRing reduces to the isogeny problem (Theorem 8.4). Here, the isogeny problem refers to the problem of finding any isogeny between two elliptic curves. Previous results [EHL+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT18, Wes22b] only applied to isogenies of smooth degree (like the ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny path problem), and were conditional on the generalised Riemann hypothesis. This is the object of Section 8.3. • There is an algorithm solving EndRing in expected time O~⁢(p1/2)~𝑂superscript𝑝12\tilde{O}(p^{1/2})over~ start_ARG italic_O end_ARG ( italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 1 / 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) (Theorem 8.5), where p>0𝑝0p>0italic_p > 0 is the characteristic. Previous algorithms were conditional on the generalised Riemann hypothesis (via the conditional equivalence with the ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny path problem [Wes22b]; see also [FIK+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT23, Theorem 5.7] for a more direct approach). Previous unconditional algorithms ran in time O~⁢(p)~𝑂𝑝\tilde{O}(p)over~ start_ARG italic_O end_ARG ( italic_p ) and only returned a full-rank subring [Koh96, Theorem 75]. This is the object of Section 8.4. Our main technical tool is an equidistribution result for isogeny walks in the graph of supersingular elliptic curves equipped with an endomorphism modulo N𝑁Nitalic_N. In fact, we prove a more general equidistribution result generalising the classical one (see [Mes86, Piz90] and Proposition 2), which we think is of independent interest. We state this result informally here, refering the reader to the body of the paper for a formal statement. Definition 1 Equipping the set of supersingular elliptic curves with extra data consists in defining for each such curve E𝐸Eitalic_E a finite set ℱ⁢(E)ℱ𝐸\mathcal{F}(E)caligraphic_F ( italic_E ), and for every isogeny φ:E→E′:𝜑→𝐸superscript𝐸′\varphi\colon E\to E^{\prime}italic_φ : italic_E → italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT a map ℱ⁢(φ):ℱ⁢(E)→ℱ⁢(E′):ℱ𝜑→ℱ𝐸ℱsuperscript𝐸′\mathcal{F}(\varphi)\colon\mathcal{F}(E)\to\mathcal{F}(E^{\prime})caligraphic_F ( italic_φ ) : caligraphic_F ( italic_E ) → caligraphic_F ( italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ), compatible under composition of isogenies (see Definitions 3 and 4). We obtain the isogeny graph 𝒢ℱsubscript𝒢ℱ\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{F}}caligraphic_G start_POSTSUBSCRIPT caligraphic_F end_POSTSUBSCRIPT of pairs (E,x)𝐸𝑥(E,x)( italic_E , italic_x ) where x∈ℱ⁢(E)𝑥ℱ𝐸x\in\mathcal{F}(E)italic_x ∈ caligraphic_F ( italic_E ) (see Definition 5). Let N≥1𝑁1N\geq 1italic_N ≥ 1 be an integer. The extra data satisfies the (mod⁢N)normal-mod𝑁(\mathrm{mod}\ N)( roman_mod italic_N )-congruence property if for every curve E𝐸Eitalic_E, pairs of endomorphisms of E𝐸Eitalic_E that are congruent modulo N𝑁Nitalic_N act identically on ℱ⁢(E)ℱ𝐸\mathcal{F}(E)caligraphic_F ( italic_E ) (see Definition 6). Our equidistribution result, stated informally, reads as follows. Theorem 1.2 Let N≥1𝑁1N\geq 1italic_N ≥ 1 be an integer. Random walks in the isogeny graph of supersingular elliptic curves equipped with extra data satisfying the (mod⁢N)normal-mod𝑁(\mathrm{mod}\ N)( roman_mod italic_N )-congruence property equidistribute optimally. We refer to Theorem 3.1 for a formal statement. The optimality refers to the fact that the graphs can be disconnected or multipartite, resulting in the adjacency matrix having several forced eigenvalues (see Proposition 3 and Remark 4), but all the remaining eigenvalues are as small as possible. A similar general result was recently proved by Codogni and Lido [CL23], so we point out some similarities and differences. In [CL23], the extra data needs to be expressed in terms of N𝑁Nitalic_N-torsion points (a level structure), whereas we allow for extra data of arbitrary nature, only requiring it to satisfy a simple property (the (mod⁢N)mod𝑁{(\mathrm{mod}\ N)}( roman_mod italic_N )-congruence property). We hope that this makes our theorem flexible, and easy to use in a variety of situations. In particular, the extra data used in our main application trivially fits within our framework; in contrast, this data is not a level structure, so does not directly fit the framework of [CL23]. Moreover, we allow p𝑝pitalic_p to divide N𝑁Nitalic_N, contrary to the results in [CL23]. Both proofs use Deligne’s bounds, but the proof in [CL23] is purely algebro-geometric, whereas ours proceeds via the Deuring correspondence and the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence; as a result, the two proofs could have different interesting generalisations. 1.2 Technical overview The ideas behind our reduction are as follows. Assume we have an oracle 𝒪𝒪\mathscr{O}script_O for OneEnd and we want to compute End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ) for a given E𝐸Eitalic_E. The ring End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ) is a lattice of dimension 4444 and volume p/4𝑝4p/4italic_p / 4. Computing End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ) consists in finding a basis: four endomorphisms that generate all the others. Given a collection of endomorphisms, one can compute the volume of the lattice they generate, and easily check whether they generate End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ). A first flawed attempt. We thus need a way to generate several endomorphisms of E𝐸Eitalic_E. Naively, one could repeatedly call 𝒪⁢(E)𝒪𝐸\mathscr{O}(E)script_O ( italic_E ), hoping to eventually obtain a generating set. This can fail, for instance if the oracle is deterministic and 𝒪⁢(E)𝒪𝐸\mathscr{O}(E)script_O ( italic_E ) always returns the same endomorphism. To circumvent this issue, it was proposed in [EHL+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT18] to randomise the curve. More precisely, one constructs a richer, randomised oracle Rich𝒪superscriptRich𝒪{{\textsc{Rich}}}^{\mathscr{O}}Rich start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT script_O end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT from 𝒪𝒪\mathscr{O}script_O as follows. On input E𝐸Eitalic_E, walk randomly on the 2222-isogeny graph, resulting in an isogeny φ:E→E′:𝜑→𝐸superscript𝐸′\varphi\colon E\to E^{\prime}italic_φ : italic_E → italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT. This graph has rapid mixing properties, so E′superscript𝐸′E^{\prime}italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT is close to uniformly distributed among supersingular curves. Now, call the oracle 𝒪𝒪\mathscr{O}script_O on E′superscript𝐸′E^{\prime}italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT, to get an endomorphism β∈End⁡(E′)𝛽Endsuperscript𝐸′\beta\in\operatorname{End}(E^{\prime})italic_β ∈ roman_End ( italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ). The composition α=φ^∘β∘φ𝛼^𝜑𝛽𝜑\alpha=\hat{\varphi}\circ\beta\circ\varphiitalic_α = over^ start_ARG italic_φ end_ARG ∘ italic_β ∘ italic_φ is an endomorphism of E𝐸Eitalic_E, the output of Rich𝒪superscriptRich𝒪{{\textsc{Rich}}}^{\mathscr{O}}Rich start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT script_O end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT. With this randomisation, there is hope that calling Rich𝒪superscriptRich𝒪{{\textsc{Rich}}}^{\mathscr{O}}Rich start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT script_O end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT repeatedly on E𝐸Eitalic_E could yield several independent endomorphisms that would eventually generate End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ). This method is essentially [EHL+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT18, Algorithm 8]. In that article, it is heuristically assumed that endomorphisms produced by Rich𝒪superscriptRich𝒪{{\textsc{Rich}}}^{\mathscr{O}}Rich start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT script_O end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT are very nicely distributed, and they deduce that a generating set for End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ) is rapidly obtained. This heuristic has a critical flaw: one can construct oracles that contradict it. Consider an integer M>1𝑀1M>1italic_M > 1, and suppose that for any input E𝐸Eitalic_E, the oracle 𝒪𝒪\mathscr{O}script_O returns an endomorphism from the strict subring 𝐙+M⁢End⁡(E)𝐙𝑀End𝐸\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}+M\operatorname{End}(E)bold_Z + italic_M roman_End ( italic_E ). Then, the above algorithm would fail, because the randomisation Rich𝒪superscriptRich𝒪{{\textsc{Rich}}}^{\mathscr{O}}Rich start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT script_O end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT would still be stuck within the subring 𝐙+M⁢End⁡(E)𝐙𝑀End𝐸\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}+M\operatorname{End}(E)bold_Z + italic_M roman_End ( italic_E ). Worse, juggling with several related integers M𝑀Mitalic_M, we will see that there are oracles for which this algorithm only stabilises after an exponential time. Identifying and resolving obstructions. The core of our method rests on the idea that this issue is, in essence, the only possible obstruction. The key is invariance by conjugation. From there, one can prove that the output of Rich𝒪superscriptRich𝒪{{\textsc{Rich}}}^{\mathscr{O}}Rich start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT script_O end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT follows a distribution that is invariant by conjugation: each output is as likely as any of its conjugates, modulo odd integers N𝑁Nitalic_N (up to some bound). Intuitively, for the outputs of Rich𝒪superscriptRich𝒪{{\textsc{Rich}}}^{\mathscr{O}}Rich start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT script_O end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT to be “stuck” in a subring (such as 𝐙+M⁢End⁡(E)𝐙𝑀End𝐸\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}+M\operatorname{End}(E)bold_Z + italic_M roman_End ( italic_E ) above), that subring must itself be stable by conjugation (modulo odd integers N𝑁Nitalic_N). There comes the next key: every subring of End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ) (of finite index not divisible by p𝑝pitalic_p) stable by conjugation modulo all integers is of the form 𝐙+M⁢End⁡(E)𝐙𝑀End𝐸\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}+M\operatorname{End}(E)bold_Z + italic_M roman_End ( italic_E ). From a basis of 𝐙+M⁢End⁡(E)𝐙𝑀End𝐸\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}+M\operatorname{End}(E)bold_Z + italic_M roman_End ( italic_E ), it is easy to recover a basis of End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ) essentially by dividing by M𝑀Mitalic_M (using a method due to Robert [Rob22] that stems from the attacks on SIDH). This intuition does not immediately translate into an algorithm, as an oracle could be “bad” without really being stuck in a subring. Imagine an oracle that outputs an element of 𝐙+2e⁢End⁡(E)𝐙superscript2𝑒End𝐸\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}+2^{e}\operatorname{End}(E)bold_Z + 2 start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_e end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT roman_End ( italic_E ) (and not in 𝐙+2e+1⁢End⁡(E)𝐙superscript2𝑒1End𝐸\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}+2^{e+1}\operatorname{End}(E)bold_Z + 2 start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_e + 1 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT roman_End ( italic_E )) with probability 2e−nsuperscript2𝑒𝑛2^{e-n}2 start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_e - italic_n end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT for each e∈[0,…,n−1]𝑒0…𝑛1e\in[0,\dots,n-1]italic_e ∈ [ 0 , … , italic_n - 1 ]. A sequence of samples (αi)isubscriptsubscript𝛼𝑖𝑖(\alpha_{i})_{i}( italic_α start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ) start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT could eventually generate End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ), but only after an amount of time exponential in n𝑛nitalic_n. This particular case could be resolved as follows: for each sample α𝛼\alphaitalic_α, identify the largest e𝑒eitalic_e such that β=(2⁢α−Tr⁡(α))/2e𝛽2𝛼Tr𝛼superscript2𝑒\beta=(2\alpha-\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha))/2^{e}italic_β = ( 2 italic_α - roman_Tr ( italic_α ) ) / 2 start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_e end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT is an endomorphism. A sequence of samples (βi)isubscriptsubscript𝛽𝑖𝑖(\beta_{i})_{i}( italic_β start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ) start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_i end_POSTSUBSCRIPT could rapidly generate 𝐙+2⁢End⁡(E)𝐙2End𝐸\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}+2\operatorname{End}(E)bold_Z + 2 roman_End ( italic_E ), from which one easily recovers End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ). This resolution first identifies the prime 2222 as the source of the obstruction, then “reduces” each sample “at 2222”. In general, such obstructive primes would appear as factors of disc⁡(α)disc𝛼\operatorname{disc}(\alpha)roman_disc ( italic_α ). Identifying these primes, and ensuring that each sample is “reduced” at each of them, one gets, in principle, a complete algorithm. However, factoring disc⁡(α)disc𝛼\operatorname{disc}(\alpha)roman_disc ( italic_α ) could be hard. Instead, we implement an optimistic approach: we identify obstructive pseudo-primes using a polynomial time partial-factoring algorithm. The factors may still be composite, but it is fine: the algorithm will either behave as if they were prime, or reveal a new factor. Equidistribution in isogeny graphs. The technical core of our result is the proof that the distribution of Rich𝒪superscriptRich𝒪{{\textsc{Rich}}}^{\mathscr{O}}Rich start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT script_O end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT is indeed invariant by conjugation. It is a consequence of Theorem 1.2, our general equidistribution result. The proof of Theorem 1.2 proceeds as follows. We use a categorical version of the Deuring correspondence to bring everything to the quaternion world. We then use a technical result (Theorem 3.2) to show that extra data satisfying the congruence property yield graphs isomorphic to special ones constructed from quaternionic groups. Finally, these special graphs are directly related to automorphic forms, so we can apply the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence and Deligne’s bounds on coefficients of modular forms. The resulting bounds on the eigenvalues of the adjacency operators give the desired fast mixing result. For any prime ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ, we write 𝐙ℓsubscript𝐙ℓ\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}bold_Z start_POSTSUBSCRIPT roman_ℓ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT and 𝐐ℓsubscript𝐐ℓ\operatorname{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}bold_Q start_POSTSUBSCRIPT roman_ℓ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT for the ring of ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-adic integers and the field of ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-adic numbers. For any prime power q𝑞qitalic_q, we write 𝐅qsubscript𝐅𝑞\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{q}bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_q end_POSTSUBSCRIPT for the finite field with q𝑞qitalic_q elements. An elliptic curve over a field K𝐾Kitalic_K is a genus 1111 projective curve with a specified base point O𝑂Oitalic_O. An elliptic curve has a unique group law (defined algebraically) with neutral element O𝑂Oitalic_O. An algebraic morphism between elliptic curves (preserving the base point) is automatically a group morphism, and is either a constant map or has a finite kernel. In the latter case, we say that the morphism is an isogeny. The degree of an isogeny φ𝜑\varphiitalic_φ, written deg⁡(φ)degree𝜑\deg(\varphi)roman_deg ( italic_φ ), is its degree as a rational map. If φ𝜑\varphiitalic_φ is separable, we have deg⁡(φ)=#⁢ker⁡(φ)degree𝜑#kernel𝜑\deg(\varphi)=\#\ker(\varphi)roman_deg ( italic_φ ) = # roman_ker ( italic_φ ). An isogeny of degree d𝑑ditalic_d is also called a d𝑑ditalic_d-isogeny. For every integer n≠0𝑛0n\neq 0italic_n ≠ 0, the multiplication-by-n𝑛nitalic_n map [n]:E→E:delimited-[]𝑛→𝐸𝐸[n]\colon E\to E[ italic_n ] : italic_E → italic_E is an isogeny of degree n2superscript𝑛2n^{2}italic_n start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT. An endomorphism of an elliptic curve E𝐸Eitalic_E is a morphism from E𝐸Eitalic_E to E𝐸Eitalic_E. When we see a nonzero endomorphism α∈End⁡(E)𝛼End𝐸\alpha\in\operatorname{End}(E)italic_α ∈ roman_End ( italic_E ) as a quaternion a∈B𝑎𝐵a\in Bitalic_a ∈ italic_B, we have deg⁡(α)=nrd⁡(a)degree𝛼nrd𝑎\deg(\alpha)=\operatorname{nrd}(a)roman_deg ( italic_α ) = roman_nrd ( italic_a ) and Tr⁡(α)=trd⁡(a)Tr𝛼trd𝑎\operatorname{Tr}(\alpha)=\operatorname{trd}(a)roman_Tr ( italic_α ) = roman_trd ( italic_a ). 2.4 Computing with isogenies Let us formalise how one can computationally encode isogenies. All we need is a notion of efficient representation: some data efficiently represents an isogeny if it allows to evaluate it efficiently on arbitrary inputs. Definition 2 (Efficient representation) Let 𝒜𝒜\mathscr{A}script_A be an algorithm, and let φ:E→E′:𝜑→𝐸superscript𝐸′\varphi:E\to E^{\prime}italic_φ : italic_E → italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT be an isogeny over a finite field 𝐅qsubscript𝐅𝑞\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{q}bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_q end_POSTSUBSCRIPT. An efficient representation of φ𝜑\varphiitalic_φ (with respect to 𝒜𝒜\mathscr{A}script_A) is some data Dφ∈{0,1}*subscript𝐷𝜑superscript01D_{\varphi}\in\{0,1\}^{*}italic_D start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_φ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ∈ { 0 , 1 } start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT * end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT such that • Dφ∈{0,1}*subscript𝐷𝜑superscript01D_{\varphi}\in\{0,1\}^{*}italic_D start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_φ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ∈ { 0 , 1 } start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT * end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT has size polynomial in log⁡(deg⁡(φ))degree𝜑\log(\deg(\varphi))roman_log ( roman_deg ( italic_φ ) ) and log⁡q𝑞\log qroman_log italic_q, and • on input Dφsubscript𝐷𝜑D_{\varphi}italic_D start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_φ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT and P∈E⁢(𝐅qk)𝑃𝐸subscript𝐅superscript𝑞𝑘P\in E(\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{q^{k}})italic_P ∈ italic_E ( bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_q start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT italic_k end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ), the algorithm 𝒜𝒜\mathscr{A}script_A returns φ⁢(P)𝜑𝑃\varphi(P)italic_φ ( italic_P ), and runs in polynomial time in log⁡(deg⁡(φ))degree𝜑\log(\deg(\varphi))roman_log ( roman_deg ( italic_φ ) ), log⁡q𝑞\log qroman_log italic_q, and k𝑘kitalic_k. Remark 1 When we say that an isogeny is in efficient representation, the algorithm 𝒜𝒜\mathscr{A}script_A is often left implicit. There are only a handful of known algorithms to evaluate isogenies, so one can think of 𝒜𝒜\mathscr{A}script_A as an algorithm that implements each of these, and Dφsubscript𝐷𝜑D_{\varphi}italic_D start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_φ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT would start with an indicator of which algorithm to use. We will use the following proposition. Proposition 1 There is an algorithm Divide which takes as input • a supersingular elliptic curve E/𝐅p2𝐸subscript𝐅superscript𝑝2E/\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{p^{2}}italic_E / bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT, • an endomorphism α𝛼\alphaitalic_α of E𝐸Eitalic_E in efficient representation, and • an integer N𝑁Nitalic_N, and returns an efficient representation of α/N𝛼𝑁\alpha/Nitalic_α / italic_N if α∈N⁢End⁡(E)𝛼𝑁normal-End𝐸\alpha\in N\operatorname{End}(E)italic_α ∈ italic_N roman_End ( italic_E ), and ⊥bottom\bot⊥ otherwise, and runs in time polynomial in the length of the input. Proof This is the division algorithm introduced by Robert [Rob22] that was derived from the attacks on SIDH [CD23, MMP+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT23, Rob23]. Note that in [Rob22], the algorithm is only presented for particular endomorphisms (translates of the Frobenius), but it works, mostly unchanged, in all generality. The general statement and detailed proof can be found in [HLMW23]. ∎ 2.5 Computational problems The endomorphism ring problem is the following. Problem 2.1 (EndRing) Given a prime p𝑝pitalic_p and a supersingular elliptic curve E𝐸Eitalic_E over 𝐅p2subscript𝐅superscript𝑝2\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{p^{2}}bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT, find four endomorphisms in efficient representation that form a basis of End⁡(E)normal-End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ) as a lattice. As the endomorphism ring problem asks to find, in a sense, all the endomorphisms, it is natural to study the problem of finding even a single one. Scalar multiplications [m]delimited-[]𝑚[m][ italic_m ] for m∈𝐙𝑚𝐙m\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}italic_m ∈ bold_Z are trivial to find, so we exclude them. Problem 2.2 (OneEnd) Given a prime p𝑝pitalic_p and a supersingular elliptic curve E𝐸Eitalic_E over 𝐅p2subscript𝐅superscript𝑝2\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{p^{2}}bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT, find an endomorphism in End⁡(E)∖𝐙normal-End𝐸𝐙\operatorname{End}(E)\setminus\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}roman_End ( italic_E ) ∖ bold_Z in efficient representation. There exists arbitrarily large endomorphisms, so it is convenient to introduce a bounded version of this problem. Given a function λ:𝐙>0→𝐙>0:𝜆→subscript𝐙absent0subscript𝐙absent0\lambda\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}_{>0}\to\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}_{>0}italic_λ : bold_Z start_POSTSUBSCRIPT > 0 end_POSTSUBSCRIPT → bold_Z start_POSTSUBSCRIPT > 0 end_POSTSUBSCRIPT, the OneEndλsubscriptOneEnd𝜆{{\textsc{OneEnd}}}_{\lambda}OneEnd start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_λ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT problem denotes the OneEnd problem where the solution α𝛼\alphaitalic_α is required to satisfy log⁡(deg⁡α)≤λ⁢(log⁡p)degree𝛼𝜆𝑝\log(\deg\alpha)\leq\lambda(\log p)roman_log ( roman_deg italic_α ) ≤ italic_λ ( roman_log italic_p ) (in other words, the length of the output is bounded by a function of the length of the input). The ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny path problem is a standard problem in isogeny-based cryptography. Fix a prime ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ. An ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny path is a sequence of isogenies of degree ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ such that the target of each isogeny is the source of the next. Random walks play a key role in isogeny-based cryptography. Fix a field 𝐅p2subscript𝐅superscript𝑝2\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{p^{2}}bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT and a prime number ℓ≠pℓ𝑝\ell\neq proman_ℓ ≠ italic_p. The supersingular ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny graph has vertices the (finitely many) isomorphism classes of supersingular elliptic curves over 𝐅p2subscript𝐅superscript𝑝2\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{p^{2}}bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT, and edges are the ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogenies between them (up to isomorphism of the target). At the heart of the Charles–Goren–Lauter hash function [CLG09], one of the first isogeny-based constructions, lies the fact that random walks in supersingular ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny graphs have rapid-mixing properties: they are Ramanujan graphs. This is the following well-known proposition. It is a particular case of our more general Theorem 3.1. Proposition 2 Let E𝐸Eitalic_E be a supersingular elliptic curve over 𝐅p2subscript𝐅superscript𝑝2\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{p^{2}}bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT, and ℓ≠pnormal-ℓ𝑝\ell\neq proman_ℓ ≠ italic_p a prime number. Let ε>0𝜀0\varepsilon>0italic_ε > 0. Proof This is a standard consequence of Pizer’s proof that the supersingular ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny graph is Ramanujan [Piz90]. Details can be found, for instance, in [BCC+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT23, Theorem 11] for the length of the walk, and in [BCC+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT23, Theorem 7, Item 2] for the description of the stationary distribution. ∎ The stationary distribution is at statistical distance O⁢(1/p)𝑂1𝑝O(1/p)italic_O ( 1 / italic_p ) of the uniform distribution. Note that rejection sampling allows to efficiently transform a sampler for the stationary distribution into a sampler for the uniform distribution. 2.7 Categories A general reference for this section is [ML98]. The reader willing to admit Theorem 3.1 without proof does not need background on automorphic forms. A general reference for this section is [JL70]; a more gentle one, for Borel-type level, is [DV13, Section 3]. Let V𝑉Vitalic_V be a finite-dimensional Hilbert space. A normal operator is an operator that commutes with its adjoint. The operator norm of an operator T𝑇Titalic_T is maxv≠0⁡‖T⁢v‖‖v‖.subscript𝑣0norm𝑇𝑣norm𝑣\max_{v\neq 0}\frac{\|Tv\|}{\|v\|}.roman_max start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_v ≠ 0 end_POSTSUBSCRIPT divide start_ARG ∥ italic_T italic_v ∥ end_ARG start_ARG ∥ italic_v ∥ end_ARG . Every normal operator stabilises the orthogonal complement of every stable subspace, is diagonalisable in an orthogonal basis, and has operator norm equal to the maximum absolute value of its eigenvalues. For ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ that do not divide N𝑁Nitalic_N, the Hecke operators Tℓsubscript𝑇ℓT_{\ell}italic_T start_POSTSUBSCRIPT roman_ℓ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT are normal operators that pairwise commute. 3 Equidistribution of elliptic curves with extra data The goal of this section is to prove Theorem 3.1. We state our results in Subsection 3.1. In Subsection 3.2, we set up a suitable version of the Deuring correspondence. The goal of Subsection 3.3 is to prove a technical result classifying extra data satisfying a simple property. In Subsection 3.4 we apply automorphic methods to prove the equidistribution theorem. 3.1 Statement of the equidistribution theorem In order to avoid bad primes, we will need to restrict the possible degrees of isogenies under consideration. Let ΣΣ\Sigmaroman_Σ be a set of primes, and let N≥1𝑁1N\geq 1italic_N ≥ 1 be an integer not divisible by any prime in ΣΣ\Sigmaroman_Σ. Definition 4 Let SSΣ⁡(p)subscriptSSΣ𝑝\operatorname{SS}_{\Sigma}(p)roman_SS start_POSTSUBSCRIPT roman_Σ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ( italic_p ) denote the category with • objects: supersingular elliptic curves over 𝐅¯psubscript¯𝐅𝑝\overline{\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}}_{p}over¯ start_ARG bold_F end_ARG start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_p end_POSTSUBSCRIPT; • morphisms HomΣ⁡(E,E′)subscriptHomΣ𝐸superscript𝐸′\operatorname{Hom}_{\Sigma}(E,E^{\prime})roman_Hom start_POSTSUBSCRIPT roman_Σ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ( italic_E , italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ): isogenies with degree a product of the primes in ΣΣ\Sigmaroman_Σ. Our results are expressed in terms of categories of elements of various functors, as in Definition 3. For us, this is going to play the role of “equipping with extra structure”: when ℱ:𝒞→Sets:ℱ→𝒞Sets\mathcal{F}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Sets}caligraphic_F : caligraphic_C → roman_Sets is a functor, El⁡(ℱ)Elℱ\operatorname{El}(\mathcal{F})roman_El ( caligraphic_F ) is the category of “objects c∈𝒞𝑐𝒞c\in\mathcal{C}italic_c ∈ caligraphic_C with extra structure taken from ℱ⁢(c)ℱ𝑐\mathcal{F}(c)caligraphic_F ( italic_c )”. A related definition can be found in [LM23, Definition 2.1], formulated at the level of graphs. For us, an advantage of the category-theoretic formulation is that we can forget about SS⁡(p)SS𝑝\operatorname{SS}(p)roman_SS ( italic_p ) and work in a quaternionic category, thanks to the Deuring correspondence. Hence Theorem 3.1 recovers [BCC+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT23, Theorem 8]. 3.2 Adélic Deuring correspondence Since automorphic forms are usually defined using adélic language, we will reformulate the Deuring correspondence using adèles (Corollary 1). The following terminology will be convenient. Definition 7 A degree map on a category 𝒞𝒞\mathcal{C}caligraphic_C is the data, for every morphism f𝑓fitalic_f of 𝒞𝒞\mathcal{C}caligraphic_C, of an integer deg⁡(f)∈𝐙≥0degree𝑓subscript𝐙absent0\deg(f)\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}_{\geq 0}roman_deg ( italic_f ) ∈ bold_Z start_POSTSUBSCRIPT ≥ 0 end_POSTSUBSCRIPT such that deg⁡(f⁢g)=deg⁡(f)⁢deg⁡(g)degree𝑓𝑔degree𝑓degree𝑔\deg(fg)=\deg(f)\deg(g)roman_deg ( italic_f italic_g ) = roman_deg ( italic_f ) roman_deg ( italic_g ) for every morphisms f,g𝑓𝑔f,gitalic_f , italic_g that can be composed, and such that deg⁡(idx)=1degreesubscriptid𝑥1\deg(\operatorname{id}_{x})=1roman_deg ( roman_id start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_x end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ) = 1 for all x∈𝒞𝑥𝒞x\in\mathcal{C}italic_x ∈ caligraphic_C. A functor ℱℱ\mathcal{F}caligraphic_F between categories equipped with degree maps is degree-preserving if deg⁡(ℱ⁢(f))=deg⁡(f)degreeℱ𝑓degree𝑓\deg(\mathcal{F}(f))=\deg(f)roman_deg ( caligraphic_F ( italic_f ) ) = roman_deg ( italic_f ) for every morphism f𝑓fitalic_f. When 𝒞𝒞\mathcal{C}caligraphic_C is a category with a degree map and ℱ:𝒞→Sets:ℱ→𝒞Sets\mathcal{F}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Sets}caligraphic_F : caligraphic_C → roman_Sets is a functor, we equip the category of elements El⁡(ℱ)Elℱ\operatorname{El}(\mathcal{F})roman_El ( caligraphic_F ) with its inherited degree map. Remark 5 In a category with a degree map, every isomorphism has degree 1111. This is not needed in our applications. We can finally prove Theorem 3.1 and Proposition 3. The quality of this enrichment is quantified in Theorem 4.1. The proof is an application of the equidistribution results of Section 3. The following lemma relates conjugation-invariance of distributions to the abstract setup of Section 3. The discriminant quadratic form on M2⁢(𝐅ℓ)/𝐅ℓsubscript𝑀2subscript𝐅ℓsubscript𝐅ℓM_{2}(\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{\ell})/\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{\ell}italic_M start_POSTSUBSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ( bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT roman_ℓ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ) / bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT roman_ℓ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT is isomorphic to x2−y⁢zsuperscript𝑥2𝑦𝑧x^{2}-yzitalic_x start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT - italic_y italic_z, so the maximal dimension of a totally isotropic subspace is 1111. The converse is Nakayama’s lemma. ∎ 5.2 Dealing with hard-to-factor numbers In the previous section, we have studied the properties of conjugacy-invariant distributions locally at a prime ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ. However, in our application, we may be confronted to local obstructions at an integer N𝑁Nitalic_N which is hard to factor; it is then not possible to isolate the primes ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ to apply the results of the previous section. In this section, fix a positive integer N𝑁Nitalic_N. We imagine that N𝑁Nitalic_N is hard to factor, and rework the previous results “locally at N𝑁Nitalic_N”. We suppose that B𝐵Bitalic_B does not ramify at any prime factor of N𝑁Nitalic_N. Recall that 𝒪⊂B𝒪𝐵\mathcal{O}\subset Bcaligraphic_O ⊂ italic_B is a maximal order. The overall strategy is folklore, but only a crucial new ingredient allows it to work in polynomial time: the division algorithm due to Robert [Rob22] (see Proposition 1). Let us start with saturation, which is used to deal with problematic primes in the main reduction. The running time of SaturateℓsubscriptSaturateℓ{{\textsc{Saturate}}}_{\ell}Saturate start_POSTSUBSCRIPT roman_ℓ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT is polynomial in ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ, not in log⁡ℓℓ\log\ellroman_log roman_ℓ, so we only use it for small ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ. (SaturateℓsubscriptSaturatenormal-ℓ{{\textsc{Saturate}}}_{\ell}Saturate start_POSTSUBSCRIPT roman_ℓ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT) is correct and runs in time polynomial in ℓnormal-ℓ\ellroman_ℓ and the size of the input. Proof Since ℓ≠pℓ𝑝\ell\neq proman_ℓ ≠ italic_p, the discriminant of the maximal order End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ) is coprime to ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ. Therefore, at each iteration, there is at least one L′superscript𝐿′L^{\prime}italic_L start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT that is contained in End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ), so that the division succeeds. Every iteration divides the discriminant by ℓ2superscriptℓ2\ell^{2}roman_ℓ start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT, so the number of iterations is half the valuation of disc⁡(R0)discsubscript𝑅0\operatorname{disc}(R_{0})roman_disc ( italic_R start_POSTSUBSCRIPT 0 end_POSTSUBSCRIPT ) at ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ, which is polynomial. At every iteration, there are O⁢(ℓ3)𝑂superscriptℓ3O(\ell^{3})italic_O ( roman_ℓ start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 3 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) lattices L′superscript𝐿′L^{\prime}italic_L start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT since they correspond exactly to lines ℓ⁢L′/ℓ⁢L⊆L/ℓ⁢Lℓsuperscript𝐿′ℓ𝐿𝐿ℓ𝐿\ell L^{\prime}/\ell L\subseteq L/\ell Lroman_ℓ italic_L start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT / roman_ℓ italic_L ⊆ italic_L / roman_ℓ italic_L. Every operation performed in the loops takes polynomial time. This proves that the algorithm terminates within the claimed running time. Theorem 7.1 (EndRing reduces to OneEnd) Algorithm 5 is a reduction from EndRing to OneEndλsubscriptOneEnd𝜆{{\textsc{OneEnd}}}_{\lambda}OneEnd start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_λ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT of expected polynomial time in log⁡(p)𝑝\log(p)roman_log ( italic_p ) and λ⁢(log⁡p)𝜆𝑝\lambda(\log p)italic_λ ( roman_log italic_p ). Proof The correctness is clear as at any time, R𝑅Ritalic_R is a subring of End⁡(E)End𝐸\operatorname{End}(E)roman_End ( italic_E ), and the success condition [End(E):R]=1[\operatorname{End}(E):R]=1[ roman_End ( italic_E ) : italic_R ] = 1 implies R=End⁡(E)𝑅End𝐸R=\operatorname{End}(E)italic_R = roman_End ( italic_E ). SQIsign, and its variant SQIsignHD [DLRW23], offer the most compact public keys and signatures of all known post-quantum constructions. Note that the results from this section apply equally to SQIsign and SQIsignHD. This digital signature scheme is constructed as an identification protocol, turned into a signature by the Fiat–Shamir transform. The protocol proves knowledge of a witness for a problem that closely resembles OneEnd. While [DKL+{}^{+}start_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT + end_FLOATSUPERSCRIPT20] heuristically argues that the protocol is sound if EndRing is hard, our main theorem allows us to prove it. Let SQIsign.param be the SQIsign public parameter generation procedure, which on input a security level k𝑘kitalic_k, outputs data 𝗉𝗉𝗉𝗉\mathsf{pp}sansserif_pp which encodes, among other things, a prime number p=Θ⁢(22⁢k)𝑝Θsuperscript22𝑘p=\Theta(2^{2k})italic_p = roman_Θ ( 2 start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 italic_k end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ). Let SQIsign.keygen be the SQIsign key generation procedure, which on input 𝗉𝗉𝗉𝗉\mathsf{pp}sansserif_pp, outputs a pair (𝗉𝗄,𝗌𝗄)𝗉𝗄𝗌𝗄(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})( sansserif_pk , sansserif_sk ). The public key 𝗉𝗄𝗉𝗄\mathsf{pk}sansserif_pk is a supersingular elliptic curve over 𝐅p2subscript𝐅superscript𝑝2\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{p^{2}}bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT, and 𝗌𝗄𝗌𝗄\mathsf{sk}sansserif_sk is its endomorphism ring. Let 𝒱𝒱\mathscr{V}script_V be an honest verifier for the SQIsign identification protocol. The same technique shows that EndRing is equivalent to the problem of finding isogenies of smooth degree. Lifting this restriction yields the more general Isogeny problem. Problem 8.3 (Isogeny) Given a prime p𝑝pitalic_p and two supersingular elliptic curves E𝐸Eitalic_E and E′superscript𝐸normal-′E^{\prime}italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT over 𝐅p2subscript𝐅superscript𝑝2\operatorname{\mathbf{F}}_{p^{2}}bold_F start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT end_POSTSUBSCRIPT, find an isogeny from E𝐸Eitalic_E to E′superscript𝐸normal-′E^{\prime}italic_E start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ′ end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT in efficient representation. Given a function λ:𝐙>0→𝐙>0:𝜆→subscript𝐙absent0subscript𝐙absent0\lambda\colon\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}_{>0}\to\operatorname{\mathbf{Z}}_{>0}italic_λ : bold_Z start_POSTSUBSCRIPT > 0 end_POSTSUBSCRIPT → bold_Z start_POSTSUBSCRIPT > 0 end_POSTSUBSCRIPT, the IsogenyλsubscriptIsogeny𝜆{{\textsc{Isogeny}}}_{\lambda}Isogeny start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_λ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT problem denotes the Isogeny problem where the solution φ𝜑\varphiitalic_φ is required to satisfy log⁡(deg⁡φ)≤λ⁢(log⁡p)degree𝜑𝜆𝑝\log(\deg\varphi)\leq\lambda(\log p)roman_log ( roman_deg italic_φ ) ≤ italic_λ ( roman_log italic_p ) (the length of the output is bounded by a function of the length of the input). From previous literature, it is easy to see that Isogeny reduces to EndRing. Proposition 13 (Isogeny reduces to EndRing) Assuming the generalised Riemann hypothesis, the problem IsogenyλsubscriptIsogeny𝜆{{\textsc{Isogeny}}}_{\lambda}Isogeny start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_λ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT reduces to EndRing in probabilistic polynomial time (with respect to the length of the instance), for some function λ⁢(log⁡p)=O⁢(log⁡p)𝜆𝑝𝑂𝑝\lambda(\log p)=O(\log p)italic_λ ( roman_log italic_p ) = italic_O ( roman_log italic_p ). Proof Isogeny immediately reduces to ℓ−IsogenyPath−ℓIsogenyPath\ell-{{\textsc{IsogenyPath}}}roman_ℓ - IsogenyPath. It is already known that the ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny path problem (with paths of length O⁢(log⁡p)𝑂𝑝O(\log p)italic_O ( roman_log italic_p )) is equivalent to EndRing [Wes22b], so IsogenyλsubscriptIsogeny𝜆{{\textsc{Isogeny}}}_{\lambda}Isogeny start_POSTSUBSCRIPT italic_λ end_POSTSUBSCRIPT reduces to EndRing. ∎ The converse reduction is trickier. As a solution to Isogeny is not guaranteed to have smooth degree, previous techniques have failed to prove that it is equivalent to EndRing. Theorem 1.1 unlocks this equivalence. Better yet, contrary to previous results of this form, Theorem 8.4 below is unconditional. In particular, it implies that EndRing reduces to the ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny path problem independently of the generalised Riemann hypothesis. The fastest known algorithms have complexity in O~⁢(p1/2)~𝑂superscript𝑝12\tilde{O}(p^{1/2})over~ start_ARG italic_O end_ARG ( italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 1 / 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ), but rely on unproven assumptions such as the generalised Riemann hypothesis. With our new results, we can now prove that EndRing can be solved in time O~⁢(p1/2)~𝑂superscript𝑝12\tilde{O}(p^{1/2})over~ start_ARG italic_O end_ARG ( italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 1 / 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) unconditionally. In contrast, the previous fastest unconditional algorithm had complexity O~⁢(p)~𝑂𝑝\tilde{O}(p)over~ start_ARG italic_O end_ARG ( italic_p ) and only returned a full-rank subring of the endomorphism ring [Koh96, Theorem 75]. The first method to reach complexity O~⁢(p1/2)~𝑂superscript𝑝12\tilde{O}(p^{1/2})over~ start_ARG italic_O end_ARG ( italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 1 / 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) under the generalised Riemann hypothesis consists in reducing EndRing to ℓ−IsogenyPath−ℓIsogenyPath\ell-{{\textsc{IsogenyPath}}}roman_ℓ - IsogenyPath (via [Wes22b]), and solving ℓ−IsogenyPath−ℓIsogenyPath\ell-{{\textsc{IsogenyPath}}}roman_ℓ - IsogenyPath by a generic graph path-finding algorithm. Unconditionally, we can follow the same strategy, but using our new reduction from EndRing to ℓ−IsogenyPath−ℓIsogenyPath\ell-{{\textsc{IsogenyPath}}}roman_ℓ - IsogenyPath (Theorem 8.4). Let us start by recalling the following folklore solution to ℓ−IsogenyPath−ℓIsogenyPath\ell-{{\textsc{IsogenyPath}}}roman_ℓ - IsogenyPath. Choose the parameter n𝑛nitalic_n as in Proposition 2, so that random isogeny paths of length O⁢(log⁡p)𝑂𝑝O(\log p)italic_O ( roman_log italic_p ) reach a target at statistical distance O⁢(1/p)𝑂1𝑝O(1/p)italic_O ( 1 / italic_p ) from uniform. Proof This follows from the fact that there is an algorithm of complexity O~⁢(p1/2)~𝑂superscript𝑝12\tilde{O}\left(p^{1/2}\right)over~ start_ARG italic_O end_ARG ( italic_p start_POSTSUPERSCRIPT 1 / 2 end_POSTSUPERSCRIPT ) for the 2222-isogeny path problem (Proposition 14), and EndRing reduces to polynomially many instances of the ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ-isogeny path problem (Theorem 8.4). ∎ References [Arp23] Sarah Arpin. The interpretation of ΔΔ\Deltaroman_Δ is that one step of the corresponding random walk consists in choosing a prime ℓ<Xℓ𝑋\ell<Xroman_ℓ < italic_X uniformly at random, and then using one step of the degree ℓℓ\ellroman_ℓ random walk. For X𝑋Xitalic_X large enough, all the eigenvalues are therefore small, except for the eigenvalue 1111 corresponding to the constant function. Moreover, these approximation can be turned into good bounds under the generalised Riemann hypothesis.
